An evolving surface finite element method for the Cahn-Hilliard equation with a logarithmic potential
Charles M. Elliott and Thomas Sales

Abstract
. In this paper we study semi-discrete and fully discrete evolving surface finite element schemes for the Cahn-Hilliard equation with a logarithmic potential. Specifically we consider linear finite elements discretising space and backward Euler time discretisation. Our analysis relies on a specific geometric assumption on the evolution of the surface. Our main results are L H 1 2 L^{2}_{H^{1}} error bounds for both the semi-discrete and fully discrete schemes, and we provide some numerical results.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
65M60, 65M16, 35K67

1.
Introduction

We are interested in evolving surface finite element (ESFEM) schemes for the Cahn-Hilliard equation posed on a sufficiently smooth, closed, orientable evolving surface,
\Gamma(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The evolving surface Cahn-Hilliard equation, as formulated in
[
14
,
15
]
, is given by

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\partial^{\bullet}u+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)u=\Delta_{\Gamma}w,\\
w=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}u+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(u),\end{gathered}

subject to the initial condition
u(0)=u_{0}
for suitable initial data.
Our focus is on the (singular) logarithmic potential function

F(r):=\frac{\theta}{2\theta_{c}}F_{\log}(r)+\frac{1-r^{2}}{2},

where
F_{\log}(r):=(1-r)\log(1-r)+(1+r)\log(1+r)
, and
0<\theta<\theta_{c}
corresponds to the (assumed constant) temperature of the system, with
\theta_{c}
being some critical temperature.
The condition
0<\theta<\theta_{c}
ensures that the potential takes a double-well form, which is seen to have two minima of equal size but differing sign.
For ease of presentation we set
\theta_{c}=1
and hence
\theta\in(0,1)
.
We will also use the notation
f(r):=F_{\log}^{\prime}(r)=\log\left(\frac{1+r}{1-r}\right)
throughout.
We expand on the appropriate differential operators in the next section.

The system (
1
) is often studied by use of the Ginzburg-Landau functional,

(1.3)

\displaystyle E^{\text{CH}}[u;t]:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\frac{\varepsilon|\nabla_{\Gamma}u|^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(u),

where the constant
\varepsilon>0
is often a small coefficient associated with the width of a transition layer connecting two phase domains in which the solution takes values close to the minima of
F(\cdot)
.
On a stationary domain this is natural, as (
1
) is the
H^{-1}
gradient flow of the Ginzburg-Landau functional (see
[
6
]
for example).
It is noted in
[
32
]
that this is not the case for an arbitrary evolving surface, and on an evolving domain this functional is known to be bounded, but not necessarily monotonic.
This has been observed numerically in
[
11
,
32
,
35
]
, where the Ginzburg-Landau functional appears to converge to a periodic function on domains with periodic evolution — to the authors knowledge there are no analytic results on this phenomena.
The Ginzburg-Landau functional remains useful in the analysis nonetheless.

The Cahn-Hilliard equation originates from the work of Cahn and Hilliard,
[
17
]
, in modelling phase separation in a binary alloy.
The Cahn-Hilliard equation originally was applied to metallurgy, for example in the studying phenomenon of spinodal decomposition
[
16
,
30
]
, but also has found application outside of this field, for example in modelling the dynamics of lipid biomembranes
[
52
]
.

This has been extensively studied on a Euclidean domain, see for example
[
48
]
, and the logarithmic potential has been studied in
[
7
,
10
,
21
,
18
,
29
]
for example.
Recently there has been interest in the equation when posed on a (possibly evolving) surface as motivated by applications such as those in
[
27
,
52
]
.
We refer to
[
1
,
2
,
14
,
15
,
36
]
for recent results on the analysis.
Likewise, from a numerical perspective we refer the reader to
[
11
,
24
,
27
,
32
,
35
,
46
]
.

The breakdown of this paper is as follows.
In Section
2
we introduce some preliminary material which will be necessary for our ESFEM schemes.
Then in Section
3
we introduce the weak formulation of the Cahn-Hilliard equation and prove an error bound for a related regularisation.
In Section
4
and Section
5
we introduce, and analyse, a semi-discrete ESFEM scheme, and a fully discrete ESFEM scheme.
We provide some numerical results in Section
6
.
We have focussed only on linear finite elements in discretising space, and backward Euler time discretisation as it is known that the logarithmic potential limits the regularity properties of the solution.
For higher order (in space and/or time) ESFEM schemes for more regular problems we refer the reader to
[
35
,
45
,
47
]
.
Finally we include some material on inverse Laplacian operators in Appendix
A
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Some geometric analysis

Throughout we will consider closed, connected, oriented
C^{2}
surfaces.
Given such a surface,
\Gamma
, we denote its normal vector by
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x,t)
.
We recall from
[
22
]
that
\Gamma
partitions
\mathbb{R}^{3}
into two regions, an interior region which we denote
\Omega
, and an exterior region
\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\bar{\Omega}
.

It is known (see
[
38
,
42
]
) that for a
C^{2}
surface,
\Gamma
, the oriented distance function
d:\mathcal{N}(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{2}
function, where
\mathcal{N}(\Gamma)
is some open, tubular neighbourhood of
\Gamma
,
\mathcal{N}(\Gamma):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid|d(x)|<\epsilon_{0}\}
for some
\epsilon_{0}
.
Given a point
x\in\mathcal{N}(\Gamma)
, we may express
x
in Fermi coordinates as

(2.1)

\displaystyle x=p(x)+d(x)\boldsymbol{\nu}(p(x)),

for some unique
p(x)\in\Gamma
.
We call
p:\mathcal{N}(\Gamma)\rightarrow\Gamma
the normal projection operator.
This will be used later in the triangulation of the surface as a way of relating functions on a triangulated surface to the exact surface.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
f:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be such that we have a differentiable extension of
f
, say
\tilde{f}
, defined on an open neighbourhood of
\Gamma
.
We define the tangential gradient of
f
at
x\in\Gamma
to be

\nabla_{\Gamma}f(x)=\nabla\tilde{f}(x)-\left(\nabla\tilde{f}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\right)\boldsymbol{\nu}(x).

We may express this componentwise as

\nabla_{\Gamma}f=\left(\underline{D}_{1}f,\underline{D}_{2}f,\underline{D}_{3}f\right).

It can be shown that this expression is independent of the choice of extension,
\tilde{f}
.

We then define the Laplace-Beltrami operator of
f
to be

\Delta_{\Gamma}f=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}f=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\underline{D}_{i}\underline{D}_{i}f.

Given these definitions, we define the mean curvature,
H
, of
\Gamma
to be the tangential divergence of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
, that is for
x\in\Gamma
,
H(x):=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\underline{D}_{i}\nu_{i}(x)
.

2.1.1.
Sobolev spaces

Definition 2.2
.

For
p\in[1,\infty]
, the Sobolev space
H^{1,p}(\Gamma)
is then defined by

H^{1,p}(\Gamma):=\{f\in L^{p}(\Gamma)\mid\underline{D}_{i}f\in L^{p}(\Gamma),\ i=1,...,n+1\},

where
\underline{D}_{i}
is understood in the weak sense.
Higher order spaces (
k\in\mathbb{N}
) are defined recursively by

H^{k,p}(\Gamma):=\{f\in H^{k-1,p}(\Gamma)\mid\underline{D}_{i}f\in H^{k-1,p}(\Gamma),\ i=1,...,n+1\},

where
H^{0,p}(\Gamma):=L^{p}(\Gamma)
.
These Sobolev spaces are known to be Banach spaces when equipped with norm,

\|f\|_{H^{k,p}(\Gamma)}:=\begin{cases}\left(\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{k}\|\underline{D}^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},&p\in[1,\infty),\\
\max_{|\alpha|=1,...,k}\|\underline{D}^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)},&p=\infty,\end{cases}

where we consider all weak derivatives of order
|\alpha|
.
We use shorthand notation,
H^{k}(\Gamma):=H^{k,2}(\Gamma)
, for the case
p=2
.

Next we introduce some notation which will be used throughout.

Notation 2.3
.

For a
\mathcal{H}^{2}-
measurable set,
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we denote the
\mathcal{H}^{2}
measure of
X
by

|X|:=\mathcal{H}^{2}(X).

For a function
f\in L^{1}(X)
we denote the mean value of
f
on
X
by

\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{X}f:=\frac{1}{|X|}\int_{X}f.

We refer the reader to
[
5
,
44
]
for further details on Sobolev spaces defined on Riemannian manifolds.

2.2.
Evolving surfaces

Definition 2.4

(
C^{2}
evolving surface)
.

Let
\Gamma_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a closed, connected, orientable
C^{2}
surface and let
\Phi:\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth map such that

(1)

For all
t\in[0,T]
,

\Phi(\cdot,t):\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow\Phi(\Gamma_{0},t)=:\Gamma(t)

is a
C^{2}
diffeomorphism.

(2)

\Phi(\cdot,0)=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma_{0}}
.

Then we call the family
(\Gamma(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
a
C^{2}
evolving surface.

It follows that
\Gamma(t)
is closed, connected and orientable for all
t
.
We define the spacetime surface to be the set

\mathcal{S}_{T}=\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\Gamma(t)\times\{t\},

and given
(x,t)\in\mathcal{S}_{T}
we denote the unit normal to
\Gamma(t)
by
\boldsymbol{\nu}(x,t)
.
We will assume throughout that there is a velocity field
V\in C^{1}([0,T];C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3}))
such that for
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\Gamma_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Phi(x,t)

\displaystyle=V(\Phi(x,t),t),

\displaystyle\Phi(x,0)

\displaystyle=x.

By compactness of
\mathcal{S}_{T}
, and assumed smoothness of
V
, there is a constant
C_{V}
independent of
t
such that

\displaystyle\|V(t)\|_{C^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C_{V},

for all
t\in[0,T]
.

2.2.1.
Time-dependent Lebesgue/Bochner spaces

Next we introduce a way of relating functions on the evolving surface back to the initial surface, which will be necessary for defining the evolving function spaces.
Let
t\in[0,T]
,
\eta\in H^{m,p}(\Gamma_{0})
and
\zeta\in H^{m,p}(\Gamma(t))
for some
m=0,1,2
, and
p\in[1,\infty]
.
We define the pushforward of
\eta
by

\Phi_{t}\eta=\eta(\Phi(\cdot,t))\in H^{m,p}(\Gamma(t)),

and the pullback of
\zeta
by

\Phi_{-t}\zeta=\zeta(\Phi(\cdot,t)^{-1})\in H^{m,p}(\Gamma_{0}).

Under our assumptions on the smoothness of
\Gamma(t)
it can be shown that the pairs
(H^{m,p}(\Gamma(t)),\Phi_{t})
are compatible in the sense of
[
3
,
4
]
for
m=0,1,2
and
p\in[1,\infty]
.
Compatibility of these spaces allows to one obtain Sobolev inequalities on
\Gamma(t)
independent of
t
.

With these definitions, we can define time-dependent Bochner spaces.

Definition 2.5
.

In the following we let
X(t)
denote a Banach space dependent on
t
, for instance
H^{m,p}(\Gamma(t))
.
The space
L^{2}_{X}
consists of (equivalence classes of) functions

\displaystyle u:[0,T]\rightarrow\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}X(t)\times\{t\},

\displaystyle t\mapsto(\bar{u}(t),t),

such that
\Phi_{-(\cdot)}\bar{u}(\cdot)\in L^{2}(0,T;X(0))
.
We identify
u
with
\bar{u}
.
This space is equipped with a norm

\|u\|_{L^{2}_{X}}=\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|u(t)\|^{2}_{X(t)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

If the family
X(t)
are in fact Hilbert spaces then this norm is induced by the inner product

(u,v)_{L^{2}_{X}}=\int_{0}^{T}(u(t),v(t))_{X(t)},

for
u,v\in L^{2}_{X}
.
In this case, as justified in
[
4
]
, we make the identification
(L^{2}_{X})^{*}\cong L^{2}_{X^{*}}
, and for
X=H^{1}
we write
L^{2}_{H^{-1}}:=(L^{2}_{H^{1}})^{*}
.

One can similarly define
L^{p}_{X}
for
p\in[1,\infty]
, which is equipped with a norm

\|u\|_{L^{p}_{X}}:=\begin{cases}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\|u(t)\|^{p}_{X(t)}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},&p\in[1,\infty),\\
\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\operatorname{ess\ sup\ }}\|u(t)\|_{X(t)},&p=\infty.\end{cases}

We refer the reader to
[
3
]
for further details.

Definition 2.6

(Strong material derivative)
.

Let
u:\mathcal{S}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be sufficiently smooth, then we define the strong material time derivative as

\partial^{\bullet}u=\Phi_{t}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{-t}u\right).

As in the stationary setting, this can be generalised to define a weak material derivative.

Definition 2.7

(Weak material derivative)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}_{H^{1}}
.
A function
v\in L^{2}_{H^{-1}}
is said to be the weak material time derivative of
u
if for all
\eta\in\mathcal{D}_{H^{1}}(0,T)
we have

\int_{0}^{T}\langle v(t),\eta(t)\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Gamma(t))\times H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}=-\int_{0}^{T}\left(u(t),\partial^{\bullet}\eta(t)\right)_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}u(t)\eta(t)\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V(t),

where

\mathcal{D}_{H^{1}}(0,T):=\left\{u\in L^{2}_{H^{1}}\mid\Phi_{-t}u(t)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma_{0}))\right\}.

We abuse notation and write
v=\partial^{\bullet}u
.

We introduce shorthand notation for a function space of weakly differentiable functions to be

H^{1}_{H^{-1}}:=\{\eta\in L^{2}_{H^{-1}}\mid\partial^{\bullet}\eta\in L^{2}_{H^{-1}}\},

and more generally we may consider the space

H^{1}_{H^{k}}:=\{\eta\in L^{2}_{H^{k}}\mid\partial^{\bullet}\eta\in L^{2}_{H^{k}}\},

for
k\geq 0
.
Clearly if
u\in L^{2}_{H^{1}}
has a strong material time derivative it has a weak material time derivative, and the two coincide.

We now state a transport theorem for quantities defined on an evolving surface.
Firstly, we define the following notation for bilinear forms to be used throughout:

\displaystyle m_{*}(t;\hat{\eta},\zeta)

\displaystyle:=\langle\hat{\eta},\zeta\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Gamma(t))\times H^{1}(\Gamma(t))},

\displaystyle m(t;\eta,\zeta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta\zeta,

\displaystyle g(t;\eta,\zeta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta\zeta\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V(t),

\displaystyle a(t;\eta,\zeta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\zeta,

where
\eta,\zeta\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
,
\hat{\eta}\in H^{-1}(\Gamma(t))
.
The argument in
t
will often be omitted, as above.
For weakly differentiable functions we have the following result.

Proposition 2.8

(
[
26
, Lemma 5.2]
)
.

Let
\eta,\zeta\in L^{2}_{H^{1}}\cap H^{1}_{H^{-1}}
.
Then
t\mapsto m(\eta(t),\zeta(t))
is absolutely continuous and such that

\frac{d}{dt}m(\eta,\zeta)=m_{*}(\partial^{\bullet}\eta,\zeta)+m_{*}(\partial^{\bullet}\zeta,\eta)+g(\eta,\zeta).

Moreover, if
\eta,\zeta\in H^{1}_{H^{1}}
then
t\mapsto a_{S}(\eta(t),\zeta(t))
is absolutely continuous and such that

\frac{d}{dt}a(\eta,\zeta)=a(\partial^{\bullet}\eta,\zeta)+a(\eta,\partial^{\bullet}\zeta)+b(\eta,\zeta).

where

b(\eta,\zeta):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\mathbf{B}(V)\nabla_{\Gamma}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\zeta,

and
\mathbf{B}(V)
is a tensor given by

\mathbf{B}(V)=\left((\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)\mathrm{id}-(\nabla_{\Gamma}V+(\nabla_{\Gamma}V)^{T})\right).

2.3.
Triangulated surfaces

In this subsection we briefly discuss discretisation of (evolving) surfaces.
Much of this presentation is the same as in
[
35
]
.

2.3.1.
Construction and lifts

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a closed, oriented
C^{2}
surface.
We introduce a discretised version of this surface, denoted
\Gamma_{h}
, which we call an triangulated (or interpolated) surface.

Definition 2.9
.

We let
(x_{i})_{i=1,...,N_{h}}\subset\Gamma
be a collection of nodes used to define a set of triangles
\mathcal{T}_{h}
.
The triangulated surface,
\Gamma_{h}
, is defined by an admissible subdivision of triangles,
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, such that

\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}K=\Gamma_{h}.

If
K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
are distinct, then we have
K_{1}^{\circ}\cap K_{2}^{\circ}=\emptyset
, and if
\bar{K}_{1}\cap\bar{K}_{2}\neq\emptyset
then this intersection is either a node of the triangulation, or a line segment connecting two adjacent nodes.

For
K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
we define following quantities

h_{K}:=\mathrm{diam}(K),\quad\rho_{K}:=\sup\{\mathrm{diam}(B)\mid B\text{ is a }2-\text{dimensional ball contained in }K\}.

We assume that the subdivision
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is quasi-uniform, meaning there exists
\rho>0
such that for all
h\in(0,h_{0})

\min\{\rho_{K}\mid K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\}\geq\rho\max_{K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}}h_{K}.

Throughout this paper we work with linear Lagrange finite elements — that is our degrees of freedom are given by the point evaluations at the nodes
(x_{i})_{i=1,...,N_{h}}
.
We will denote the set of shape functions as

S_{h}:=\left\{\phi_{h}\in C(\Gamma_{h})\mid\phi_{h}|_{K}\text{ is affine linear for }K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\right\}.

The normal
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{h}
is defined piecewise on each element of
\Gamma_{h}
which gives rise to a discrete tangential gradient,
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}
, defined element-wise on
\Gamma_{h}
.

Next we relate functions on
\Gamma_{h}
and
\Gamma
by defining lifts.
We will assume that our triangulated surface
\Gamma_{h}
is such that
\Gamma_{h}\subset\mathcal{N}(\Gamma)
for
\mathcal{N}(\Gamma)
a tubular neighbourhood as described above.
This is possible in practice by considering a sufficiently fine triangulation.
This allows us to define lifts of functions.

Definition 2.10
.

For a function
\eta_{h}:\Gamma_{h}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we implicitly define the lift operation on
\eta_{h}
by

\eta_{h}^{\ell}(p(x)):=\eta_{h}(x),

where
p
is the normal projection operator (
2.1
).

Similarly, for
\eta:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we define the inverse lift by

\eta^{-\ell}(x)=\eta(p(x)).

In
[
33
]
the following result concerning lifts of functions is proven.

Lemma 2.11
.

There exists constants
C_{1},C_{2}
, independent of
h
such that for
\eta_{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h})

(2.2)

\displaystyle C_{1}\|\eta_{h}^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq C_{2}\|\eta_{h}^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},

(2.3)

\displaystyle C_{1}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\eta_{h}^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h})}\leq C_{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\eta_{h}^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}.

This shows that there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}
independent of
h
such that

C_{1}|\Gamma|\leq|\Gamma_{h}|\leq C_{2}|\Gamma|.

Another useful consequence of the stability of the lift is that it allows one to obtain a Poincaré inequality independent of
h
.

Definition 2.12
.

We say that our triangulation is exact if the lifted triangles
K^{\ell}:=\{x^{\ell}\mid x\in\Gamma_{h}\}
form a conforming subdivision of
\Gamma
.

2.3.2.
Evolving triangulated surfaces

Given an evolving surface,
(\Gamma(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
we construct an evolving triangulated surface as follows.
Firstly, we construct an admissible triangulation,
\mathcal{T}_{h}(0)
, of
\Gamma_{0}
, with nodes
(x_{i,0})_{i=1,...,N_{h}}
as above.
We denote this triangulated surface as
\Gamma_{h}(0)
.
The nodes of
\Gamma_{h}(0)
then evolve in time according to the ODE,

\frac{d}{dt}x_{i}(t)=V(x_{i}(t),t),\qquad x_{i}(0)=x_{i,0},

where
V
is the velocity field associated with the evolution of
\Gamma(t)
.
This induces a triangulation
\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
, where
K(0)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(0)
gives rise to a triangle
K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
by evolving the nodes as above.
The triangulated surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
is then defined as

\Gamma_{h}(t):=\bigcup_{K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)}K(t),

which will be admissible by construction of
\Gamma_{h}(0)
.
Here the
h
parameter is defined to be

h:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\max_{K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)}h_{K(t)}.

We denote the discrete spacetime surface as

\mathcal{S}_{h,T}:=\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\Gamma_{h}(t)\times\{t\}.

We note that as the domain is evolving, the set of basis functions also evolves in time.
As such, we denote the set of basis functions at time
t
to be

S_{h}(t)=\left\{\phi_{h}\in C(\Gamma_{h}(t))\mid\phi_{h}|_{K(t)}\text{ is affine linear },K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)\right\}.

This definition allows one to characterise the velocity of the surface
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, as an arbitrary point
x(t)\in\Gamma_{h}(t)
will evolve according to the discrete velocity,
V_{h}
, given by

\frac{d}{dt}x(t)=V_{h}(x(t),t):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}\dot{x}_{i}(t)\phi_{i}(x(t),t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}V(x_{i}(t),t)\phi_{i}(x(t),t),

where
\phi_{i}(t)
is the ‘
i
’th nodal basis function of
\Gamma_{h}(t)
.
From this we observe that
V_{h}
is the Lagrange interpolant of
V
.

The evolution of
\Gamma(t)
induces a discrete flow map
\Phi^{h}:\Gamma_{h}(0)\rightarrow\Gamma_{h}(t)
(see
[
33
]
) which one can use to define discrete pushforwards/pullbacks
\Phi_{t}^{h}\eta_{h},\Phi_{-t}^{h}\eta_{h}
, for which one defines a strong material time derivative by

\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h}=\Phi_{t}^{h}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{-t}^{h}\eta_{h}\right).

One can similarly define a weak discrete material derivative in the standard way.

An important consequence of this is the transport property of basis functions — if
\phi_{i}:\mathcal{S}_{h,T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is one of the nodal basis functions of
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, then

\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\phi_{i}=0.

This is an important property, which is exploited in implementing evolving surface finite element schemes as it eliminates any velocity terms in the fully discrete formulation.

Definition 2.13
.

The evolving triangulated surface,
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, is said to be uniformly quasi-uniform if there exists
\rho>0
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
, and
h\in(0,h_{0})
we have

\min\{\rho_{K(t)}\mid K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)\}\geq\rho h.

A useful property of uniformly quasi-uniform meshes is the following discrete Sobolev inequality

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|\phi_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\log\left(\frac{1}{h}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\phi_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))},

where
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
, and
C
is independent of
h,t
.
We do not prove this for an evolving surface, but we do refer to
[
50
]
Lemma 6.4 for a proof in the stationary, Euclidean case.

Definition 2.14
.

We say that the triangulation,
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, of
\Gamma_{h}
is acute if for all
K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
the angles of
K
are less than or equal to
\frac{\pi}{2}
.
We say that the triangulation for an evolving surface is evolving acute if
\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
is acute for all
t\in[0,T]
.

We say the evolving triangulation is exact if for all
t\in[0,T]

\bigcup_{K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)}K^{\ell}(t)=\Gamma(t).

In our analysis we make the following assumptions:

Assumption 2.15

(Evolving triangulation)
.

(1)

For all
t\in[0,T]
the evolving triangulated surface,
\Gamma_{h}(t)
is uniformly quasi-uniform.

(2)

For all
t\in[0,T]
the evolving triangulated surface,
\Gamma_{h}(t)
is exact.

(3)

For all
t\in[0,T]
the evolving triangulated surface,
\Gamma_{h}(t)
is acute.

(4)

For each
t\in[0,T]
one has
\Gamma_{h}(t)\subset\mathcal{N}(\Gamma(t))
so that we may define the lift at all times
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 2.16
.

The condition that the mesh remains acute for all time is somewhat problematic as it is known that an initially acute mesh may lose the acute property after evolution in time.
However, if the initial mesh is strictly acute then for some small time interval (dependent on the velocity field
V
) the mesh will remain strictly acute.
More generally one may want to use a remeshing procedure, such as the algorithm in
[
31
]
— where an initially acute mesh will remain acute under evolution as harmonic map flow yields conformal maps.
It is likely that one can extend the analysis in this paper to meshes which satisfy weaker conditions on the mesh, for example the Xu-Zikatanov condition
[
51
]
, but we shall not consider this here.

We now state a discrete analogue of the transport theorem, Proposition
2.8
.
Here we denote the (time-dependent) bilinear forms by

\displaystyle m_{h}(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\eta_{h}\zeta_{h},

\displaystyle a_{h}(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h},

\displaystyle g_{h}(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h},

where we typically omit the
t
argument.
We then have a discrete transport theorem for these bilinear forms.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
be such that
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\zeta_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
exist.
Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}m_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})=m_{h}(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})+m_{h}(\eta_{h},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\zeta_{h})+g_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}a_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})=a_{h}(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})+a_{h}(\eta_{h},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\zeta_{h})+b_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}).

Here

b_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\mathbf{B}_{h}(V_{h})\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h},

where

\mathbf{B}_{h}(V_{h})=\left((\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h})\mathrm{id}-(\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}V_{h}+(\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}V_{h})^{T})\right).

2.3.3.
Geometric perturbation estimates and the Ritz projection

Here we state several results which are crucial to the numerical analysis of surface PDEs.
Firstly, as noted in
[
33
]
, one can obtain another material derivative by (inverse) lifting a function onto
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, differentiating, and lifting back onto
\Gamma(t)
.

Definition 2.18
.

For a sufficiently smooth function,
\eta:\mathcal{S}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
, we define the (strong) lifted material derivative as

\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\eta=\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta^{-\ell}\right)^{\ell}.

This can also be expressed as

\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\eta=\Phi_{t}^{\ell}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{-t}^{\ell}\eta\right),

where
\Phi_{t}^{\ell},\Phi_{-t}^{\ell}
are the pushforward/pullback respectively associated to the map
\Phi^{\ell}:\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow\Gamma(t)
defined by

\Phi^{\ell}(x,t)=\Phi^{h}(x^{-\ell},t)^{\ell}.

This is discussed in detail in
[
33
]
.
From this we obtain an alternate version of Proposition
2.8
.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\eta,\zeta\in C^{1}_{L^{2}}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}m(t;\eta,\zeta)=m(t;\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\eta,\zeta)+m(t;\eta,\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\zeta)+g_{\ell}(t;\eta,\zeta),

where

g_{\ell}(t;\eta,\zeta)=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta\zeta(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V_{h}^{\ell}).

Similarly, if
\eta,\zeta\in C^{1}_{H^{1}}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}a(t;\eta,\zeta)=a(t;\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell},\zeta)+a(t;\eta,\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\zeta)+b_{\ell}(t;\eta,\zeta),

where

b_{\ell}(\eta,\zeta)=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\mathbf{B}(V_{h}^{\ell})\nabla_{\Gamma}\eta\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\zeta,

and
\mathbf{B}(V_{h}^{\ell})
is as in Proposition
2.8
.

This allows one to define a weak lifted material derivative in the usual way.
We can then related
\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}
and
\partial^{\bullet}
through the following result.

Lemma 2.20

(
[
33
, Lemma 9.25]
)
.

Let
\eta\in H^{1}_{H^{1}}
.
Then we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\bullet}\eta-\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\eta\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch^{2}\|\eta\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))},

and if we have further that
\eta\in H^{1}_{H^{2}}
, then

(2.6)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\Gamma}(\partial^{\bullet}\eta-\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}\eta)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch\|\eta\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

We now state some results which allow us to compare bilinear forms on
\Gamma(t)
and
\Gamma_{h}(t)
.
The following results are proven in
[
33
]
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
, and
h
be sufficiently small.
Then there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
t,h
, such that

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left|m\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)-m_{h}\left(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(2.8)

\displaystyle\left|a\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)-a_{h}\left(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\left|g_{\ell}\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)-g_{h}\left(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left|b_{\ell}\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)-b_{h}\left(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\left|g\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)-g_{\ell}\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ch\|\eta_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\zeta_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(2.12)

\displaystyle\left|b\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)-b_{\ell}\left(t;\eta_{h}^{\ell},\zeta_{h}^{\ell}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ch\|\eta_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\zeta_{h}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},.

Next we introduce a projection onto the shape functions which is useful in the error analysis for surface finite elements.

Definition 2.22
.

For
z\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
we define the Ritz projection
1
1

1

Some authors define the Ritz projection differently, see
[
32
, Remark 3.4]
.
,
\Pi_{h}z\in S_{h}(t)
, to be the unique solution of

(2.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} a_{h}(\Pi_{h}z,\phi_{h})&=a(z,\phi_{h}^{\ell}),\\
\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\Pi_{h}z&=\int_{\Gamma(t)}z,\end{aligned}

for all
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
.
We denote the lift of the Ritz projection by

\pi_{h}z=(\Pi_{h}z)^{\ell}.

One has the following bounds for the Ritz projection, for which we refer the reader to
[
32
,
33
]

Lemma 2.23
.

For
z\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
we have the following,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{h}z\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|z\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{h}z-z\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch\|z\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}.

Moreover, if
z\in H^{2}(\Gamma(t))
then

(2.16)

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{h}z\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}=\|\pi_{h}z\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|z\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))},

(2.17)

\displaystyle\|\pi_{h}z-z\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+h\|\nabla_{\Gamma}(\pi_{h}z-z)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch^{2}\|z\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

We also have the following lemma regarding the time derivative of the Ritz projection.

Lemma 2.24
.

For
z:\mathcal{S}_{T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
z,\partial^{\bullet}{z}\in H^{2}(\Gamma(t))
then
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\Pi_{h}z\in S_{h}(t)
exists and

(2.18)

\|\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\Pi_{h}z\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq C\left(\|z\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\partial^{\bullet}z\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\right),

and

(2.19)

\left\|\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}(\pi_{h}z-z)\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+h\left\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\partial^{\bullet}_{\ell}(\pi_{h}z-z)\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch^{2}\left(\|z\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\left\|\partial^{\bullet}z\right\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\right),

where
C
is a constant independent of
h,t
.

2.3.4.
Interpolation and numerical integration

Definition 2.25
.

We denote the linear nodal basis functions, on
\Gamma_{h}(t)
, by
\phi_{i}
.
Then the (Lagrange) interpolation operator
I_{h}:C(\Gamma_{h}(t))\rightarrow S_{h}(t)
is defined by

I_{h}z_{h}(t;x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}z_{h}(t;x_{i})\phi_{i}(t;x),

where
z_{h}\in C(\Gamma_{h}(t))
.
Similarly, we define the lifted interpolation operator,
I_{h}^{\ell}:C(\Gamma(t))\rightarrow S_{h}^{\ell}(t)
, by

I_{h}^{\ell}z(t;x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}z(t;x_{i})\phi_{i}^{\ell}(t;x),

where
z\in C(\Gamma(t))
.
We will omit the
t
argument as usual.

In
[
33
]
the following result is shown for the lifted interpolation operator, where we note that as
H^{2}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow C^{0}(\Gamma(t))
the interpolant of a
H^{2}
function is well-defined.

Lemma 2.26
.

The lifted interpolation operator defined above satisfies, for
z\in H^{2}(\Gamma(t))

(2.20)

\displaystyle\left\|z-I_{h}^{\ell}z\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+h\left\|\nabla_{\Gamma}(z-I_{h}^{\ell}z)\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch^{2}\|z\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))},

and more generally for
z\in H^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))\cap C(\Gamma(t))

(2.21)

\displaystyle\left\|z-I_{h}^{\ell}z\right\|_{L^{q}(\Gamma(t))}+h\left\|\nabla_{\Gamma}(z-I_{h}^{\ell}z)\right\|_{L^{q}(\Gamma(t))}\leq Ch^{k+\frac{2}{q}-\frac{2}{p}}\|z\|_{H^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))},

where
C
is a constant independent of
t,h
, and
k=1,2
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
.

The interpolation operator also has the following property which will be used throughout.

Lemma 2.27
.

For a monotonically increasing function
\lambda\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, and a function
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
we have that

(2.22)

\displaystyle\|I_{h}\lambda(\phi_{h})-\lambda(\phi_{h})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq Ch\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}I_{h}\lambda(\phi_{h})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

where
C
is independent of
t,h
.

Proof.

This proof is largely the same as that of
[
28
]
.
To begin, we restrict to a single element
K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
, and note that a linear function on
K(t)
attains its extrema at the nodes.
Hence there are nodes
x_{m}(t),x_{M}(t)\in\partial K(t)
such that
\phi_{h}(x_{m}(t))\leq\phi_{h}(x)\leq\phi_{h}(x_{M}(t))
for all
x\in K(t)
.
By the monotonicity assumption on
\lambda
we find

\lambda(\phi_{h}(x_{m}(t)))\leq\lambda(\phi_{h}(x))\leq\lambda(\phi_{h}(x_{M}(t))),

for all
x\in K(t)
.
From this is it clear that

\|I_{h}\lambda(\phi_{h})-\lambda(\phi_{h})\|_{L^{\infty}(K(t))}\leq\lambda(\phi_{h}(x_{M}))-\lambda(\phi_{h}(x_{m}))=|(x_{M}-x_{m})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}I_{h}\lambda(\phi_{h})|,

where the equality follows since
I_{h}\lambda
is linear on
K(t)
.
The desired inequality follows from this.
∎

The use of mass lumping in surface finite elements has previously been considered in
[
27
,
39
,
40
]
and so we do not prove every property of mass lumped finite elements that we use.
It is a well known property of acute triangulations (see for example
[
19
,
41
]
) that the stiffness matrix,
A(t)=(a_{ij}(t))_{ij}
, where

a_{ij}(t)=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\phi_{i}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\phi_{j},

is such that

a_{ii}(t)>0,\qquad a_{ij}(t)\leq 0\ (i\neq j).

In particular this means that we have the following result.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
\lambda\in C(\mathbb{R})
, with
\lambda(0)=0
, and
\lambda^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
0\leq\lambda^{\prime}(s)\leq M_{\lambda}<\infty
for almost all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then for an evolving acute triangulation of
\Gamma_{h}(t)
one has

(2.23)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}I_{h}\lambda(\phi_{h}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\leq M_{\lambda}\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\phi_{h}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}I_{h}\lambda(\phi_{h}(t)),

for all
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
.

We refer the reader to
[
20
,
49
]
for the proof, noting that the evolving surface changes nothing (provided that one can ensure the triangulation remains acute for
t\in[0,T]
).

We introduce the following shorthand notation

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}(t;\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),

for the lumped mass
L^{2}
inner product on
\Gamma_{h}(t)
.
The bilinear form
\bar{m}_{h}(\cdot,\cdot)
corresponds to the use of numerical integration in a finite element scheme.
In particular, it defines a new inner product on
S_{h}(t)
, and hence we have a new norm given by

\left\|\eta_{h}\right\|_{h,t}^{2}:=\bar{m}_{h}(t;\eta_{h},\eta_{h}),

where we will omit
t
as before.
Differentiating this bilinear form in time yields the following.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
be such that
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\zeta_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
exist.
Then

\frac{d}{dt}\bar{m}_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})=\bar{m}_{h}(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})+\bar{m}_{h}(\eta_{h},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\zeta_{h})+g_{h}(I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),1).

Proof.

By definition we have

\bar{m}_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),

and so the transport theorem yields

\frac{d}{dt}\bar{m}_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})=m_{h}(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),1)+g_{h}(I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),1).

Now writing
I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h})=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}\eta_{h}(t;x_{i}(t))\zeta_{h}(t;x_{i}(t))\phi_{i}(t)
, and using the transport property
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\phi_{i}=0
, one finds that

\partial^{\bullet}_{h}I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h})=I_{h}(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\eta_{h}\zeta_{h})+I_{h}(\eta_{h}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}\zeta_{h}),

from which the result follows.
∎

Next we adapt a result of Ciavaldini
[
20
]
to the setting of evolving surface finite elements.

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
. Then we have

(2.24)

\displaystyle\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\eta_{h}\right\|_{h,t}\leq C\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(2.25)

\displaystyle|\bar{m}_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})-m_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})|\leq

\displaystyle Ch\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(2.26)

\displaystyle|\bar{m}_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})-m_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})|\leq

\displaystyle Ch^{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

where
C
is some constant independent of
h,t
.

Proof.

By the piecewise nature of the functions, it is sufficient to restrict to a single
K(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{h}(t)
, and we denote the nodes of
K(t)
by
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
.
To show the first result, we follow the presentation of
[
20
]
.
Firstly, we note that

\left\|\eta_{h}\right\|_{h,t}^{2}=\int_{K(t)}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})^{2}\phi_{i}=\frac{|K(t)|}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})^{2},

where
\phi_{i}
is the nodal basis function associated to
x_{i}
, and we have used a quadrature rule which is exact for a linear function.
Similarly, by noting that
\eta_{h}^{2}
is piecewise quadratic, we find that

\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(K(t))}^{2}=\int_{K(t)}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})\phi_{i}\right)^{2}=\frac{|K(t)|}{6}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})^{2}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})\eta_{h}(x_{j})\right),

where we have used a quadrature rule which is exact for quadratics.
One can readily show that

\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})\eta_{h}(x_{j})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})^{2},

and hence it follows that
\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq\left\|\eta_{h}\right\|_{h,t}
.
To show the second part of (
2.24
), we use a similar argument except now we use a bound of the form

2\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})^{2}\leq M\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})^{2}+M\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 3}\eta_{h}(x_{i})\eta_{h}(x_{j}),

which one can show
2
2

2

For example one can use the identity
(M-2)(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})+M(ab+ac+bc)=(\frac{M}{2}-2)(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})+\frac{M}{2}(a+b+c)^{2}
for
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}.
holds for
M>4
.

To show (
2.26
), we firstly recall from from
[
33
, Theorem 6.13]
that

\|\chi-I_{K}\chi\|_{L^{1}(K(t))}\leq Ch_{K}^{2}|\chi|_{H^{2,1}(K(t))},

where
|\cdot|_{H^{2,1}(K(t))}
denotes the
H^{2,1}(K(t))
seminorm, and
I_{K}
is the local Lagrange interpolation operator on
K(t)
, and
C
is independent of
h,t
.
This yields

(2.27)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{K(t)}\left(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}-I_{K}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h})\right)\right|\leq\int_{K(t)}\left|\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}-I_{K}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h})\right|\leq Ch_{K}^{2}|\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}|_{H^{2,1}(K(t))}.

On
K(t)

\eta_{h},\zeta_{h}
are in fact smooth, and so the necessary derivatives exist.
Moreover, as the functions are linear on
K(t)
we observe that
\underline{D}_{i}\underline{D}_{j}\eta_{h}=\underline{D}_{i}\underline{D}_{j}\zeta_{h}=0
for
i,j=1,...,3
.
Computing the
H^{2,1}(K(t))
seminorm, and using the above observation, we find

\displaystyle|\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}|_{H^{2,1}(K(t))}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\|\underline{D}_{i}\underline{D}_{j}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h})\|_{L^{1}(K(t))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{K(t)}|\underline{D}_{i}\eta_{h}||\underline{D}_{j}\zeta_{h}|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(K(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(K(t))}.

Combining this with (
2.27
) gives a local version of (
2.26
), which one uses to deduce the global result (since our mesh is uniformly quasi-uniform).
(
2.25
) then follows from (
2.26
) and an inverse inequality.
∎

Remark 2.31
.

By arguing along the same lines one can show that

(2.28)

\displaystyle|g_{h}(\eta_{h},\zeta_{h})-g_{h}(I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),1)|\leq Ch^{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

and hence one can use an inverse inequality to see that

|g_{h}(I_{h}(\eta_{h}\zeta_{h}),1)|\leq C\|\eta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\zeta_{h}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}.

We shall use this bound repeatedly throughout.

3.
Variational formulation and well-posedness

The weak formulation of the Cahn-Hilliard equation of
[
14
,
15
]
is to find a solution pair
(u,w)\in L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}\cap H^{1}_{H^{-1}}\times L^{2}_{H^{1}}
such that

(3.1)

\displaystyle m_{*}\left(\partial^{\bullet}{u},\phi\right)+g(u,\phi)+a(w,\phi)=0,

(3.2)

\displaystyle m(w,\eta)=\varepsilon a(u,\phi)+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f(u),\phi)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(u,\phi),

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, where the initial condition
u(0)=u_{0}
holds a.e. on
\Gamma_{0}
.
The well-posedness of this weak formulation has been studied in
[
14
,
15
]
.
Due to the logarithmic potential the function
u
must be such that
|u|<1
a.e. on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
This restricts the class of admissible initial data.
One would at least expect that we require the initial data to be such that
|u_{0}|\leq 1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
In fact we require a stronger condition — that
u_{0}
is such that

m_{u_{0}}(t):=\frac{1}{|\Gamma(t)|}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{0}}u_{0}\right|<1,

for all
t\in[0,T]
.
We refer to
[
14
, Proposition 5.1]
for the explanation for how this condition arises, and to
[
15
]
for a physical interpretation.
As such, one considers the set of admissible initial conditions to be

\mathcal{I}_{0}:=\left\{\eta\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})\mid|\eta|\leq 1\ \text{a.e. on }\Gamma_{0},\ E^{\text{CH}}[\eta;0]<\infty,\ m_{\eta}(t)<1\ \forall t\in[0,T]\right\}.

In
[
14
,
15
]
it is shown that there exists a solution pair solving (
3.1
), (
3.2
), with
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
, by considering the regularised potential function defined for
\delta\in(0,1)
by

(3.3)

F_{\log}^{\delta}(r):=\begin{cases}(1-r)\log(\delta)+(1+r)\log(2-\delta)+\frac{(1-r)^{2}}{2\delta}+\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2(2-\delta)}-1,\ &r\geq 1-\delta\\
(1+r)\log(1+r)+(1-r)\log(1-r),\ &|r|<1-\delta\\
(1+r)\log(\delta)+(1-r)\log(2-\delta)+\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2\delta}+\frac{(1-r)^{2}}{2(2-\delta)}-1,\ &r\leq-1+\delta\end{cases}.

It can be shown that
F^{\delta}_{\log}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
This approach has been used in several papers on the logarithmic potential, see for example
[
7
,
21
,
29
]
.
We will adopt the shorthand notation

F^{\delta}(r)=\frac{\theta}{2}F^{\delta}_{\text{log}}(r)+\frac{1-r^{2}}{2},\quad f^{\delta}(r):=(F^{\delta}_{\log})^{\prime}(r).

It can be shown that the function
f^{\delta}
is such that for
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\ (r-s)^{2}\leq(f^{\delta}(r)-f^{\delta}(s))(r-s),

(3.5)

\displaystyle|f^{\delta}(r)-f^{\delta}(s)|\leq\frac{1}{\delta}|r-s|,

for sufficiently small
\delta
.
If also
|r|,|s|>1-\delta
then we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\delta}(r-s)^{2}\leq(f^{\delta}(r)-f^{\delta}(s))(r-s).

We end this section by proving an error bound between the exact solution, and the solution of the regularised equation.
We also recall the definition of the inverse Laplacian,
\mathcal{G}
, from Appendix
A
.
This proof is adapted from
[
7
, Theorem 2.1]
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(u,w)
denote the solution of (
3.1
), (
3.2
), and
(u^{\delta},w^{\delta})
denote the solution of the regularised problem for
\delta\in(0,1)
.
Then for sufficiently small
\delta>0
we have that

(3.7)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}(u-u^{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|u-u^{\delta}\|_{-1}^{2}\leq C\delta.

Proof.

To begin, we define
E_{u}:=u-u^{\delta},E_{w}:=w-w^{\delta}
, which one readily finds satisfies

(3.8)

\displaystyle m_{*}\left(\partial^{\bullet}{E_{u}},\phi\right)+g(E_{u},\phi)+a(E_{w},\phi)=0,

(3.9)

\displaystyle m(E_{w},\phi)=\varepsilon a(E_{u},\phi)+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),\phi)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(E_{u},\phi),

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
and almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
We note
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}E_{u}=0
, and so
\mathcal{G}E_{u}
is well defined.
Hence we test (
3.8
) with
\mathcal{G}E_{u}
and (
3.9
) with
E_{u}
, from which one readily finds that

(3.10)

\displaystyle m_{*}\left(\partial^{\bullet}{E_{u}},\mathcal{G}E_{u}\right)+g(E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u})+\varepsilon a(E_{u},E_{u})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(E_{u},E_{u})=0.

We now want to rewrite the first two terms appropriately.
Firstly we observe that

m_{*}\left(\partial^{\bullet}{E_{u}},\mathcal{G}E_{u}\right)+g(E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u})=\frac{d}{dt}m(E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u})-m(E_{u},\partial^{\bullet}\mathcal{G}E_{u}),

where we note that
\partial^{\bullet}\mathcal{G}E_{u}\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
(see Appendix
A
).
Then we see that
m(E_{u},\partial^{\bullet}\mathcal{G}E_{u})=a(\mathcal{G}E_{u},\partial^{\bullet}\mathcal{G}E_{u})
, and from the transport theorem

a(\mathcal{G}E_{u},\partial^{\bullet}\mathcal{G}E_{u})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}a(\mathcal{G}E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u})-\frac{1}{2}b(\mathcal{G}E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u}).

Hence we find that (
3.10
) becomes

(3.11)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}b(\mathcal{G}E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u})+\varepsilon a(E_{u},E_{u})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|E_{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

Now from the definition of
\mathcal{G}
and Young’s inequality

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|E_{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{3}}\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}E_{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

and likewise using the smoothness of
V
, one finds

b(\mathcal{G}E_{u},\mathcal{G}E_{u})\leq C\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}.

Next we express
m(f(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})
as

m(f(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})=m(f^{\delta}(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})-m(f^{\delta}(u)-f(u),E_{u}).

Combining these in (
3.11
) yields

(3.12)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}+\varepsilon a(E_{u},E_{u})+\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})\leq C\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}+\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(u)-f(u),E_{u}).

Hence we aim to bound the potential terms suitably to obtain the result.
Firstly, by defining the sets

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}_{\delta}(t):=\left\{x\in\Gamma(t)\ |\ 1-\delta\leq u(x,t)\leq u^{\delta}(x,t)\right\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{-}_{\delta}(t):=\left\{x\in\Gamma(t)\ |\ -1+\delta\geq u(x,t)\geq u^{\delta}(x,t)\right\},

and using the monotonicity of
f^{\delta}
and (
3.6
) we find that

m(f^{\delta}(u)-f^{\delta}(u^{\delta}),E_{u})\geq\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{\Gamma_{\delta}^{+}(t)\cup\Gamma_{\delta}^{-}(t)}E_{u}^{2}

Secondly, we can consider three cases:

(1)

For
|r|\leq 1-\delta
we have that
f^{\delta}(r)=f(r)
,

(2)

For
r\geq 1-\delta
and
s\leq r
we have
(f^{\delta}(r)-f(r))(r-s)\leq 0
,

(3)

For
r\leq-1+\delta
and
s\geq r
we have
(f^{\delta}(r)-f(r))(r-s)\leq 0
,

hence we find that

m(f^{\delta}(u)-f(u),E_{u})\leq-\int_{\Gamma_{\delta}^{+}(t)\cup\Gamma_{\delta}^{-}(t)}f(u)E_{u},

as
f^{\delta}(u)E_{u}\leq 0
on
\Gamma_{\delta}^{+}(t)\cup\Gamma_{\delta}^{-}(t)
.
and by applying Young’s inequality one finds that

\left|\int_{\Gamma_{\delta}^{+}(t)\cup\Gamma_{\delta}^{-}(t)}f(u)E_{u}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{\Gamma_{\delta}^{+}(t)\cup\Gamma_{\delta}^{-}(t)}E_{u}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}\int_{\Gamma(t)}f(u)^{2}.

Combining these bounds in (
3.12
) one finds

\varepsilon\|\nabla_{\Gamma}E_{u}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{d}{dt}\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}\leq C\|E_{u}\|_{-1}^{2}+\frac{\delta\theta}{2\varepsilon}\|f(u)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

Thus integrating in time, noting that
f(u)\in L^{2}_{L^{2}}
and applying Grönwall’s inequality yields the result.
∎

4.
Semi-discretisation of the problem

4.1.
Finite element problem

We now propose and analyse a spatially discrete numerical scheme for (
3.1
), (
3.2
).
In order to show stability of the numerical scheme we make the following assumption on the evolution of
\Gamma(t)
.

Assumption 4.1
.

We assume that the velocity,
V
, of
\Gamma(t)
is such that
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V\geq 0
for all
(x,t)\in\mathcal{S}_{T}
.

This condition has also been considered in recent work on the degenerate Cahn-Hilliard equation on an evolving surface
[
34
]
, in which the following geometric formulation of this assumption is shown.

Lemma 4.2

(
[
34
, Lemma 4.1]
)
.

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V\geq 0
if, and only if, for every
\mathcal{H}^{2}
measurable region
\Sigma(t)\subset\Gamma(t)
,

\frac{d}{dt}|\Sigma(t)|\geq 0.

As remarked in
[
34
]
if one considers a velocity which is exclusively in the normal direction then
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V=HV_{N}
.
As such that above condition holds for a normal velocity is of the form
V_{N}=g(H)
, as a function of the mean curvature, where
g(\cdot)
is sufficiently smooth, and such that
xg(x)\geq 0
.
An explicit example of such an evolution is inverse mean curvature flow,
g(H)=\frac{1}{H}
.
We refer the reader to
[
12
]
for a discussion of geometric evolution equations of form
V_{N}=g(H)
.

It is not clear that
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V\geq 0
would imply that
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
.
However, if instead we assume a strict inequality,
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V>0
, then as
V_{h}=I_{h}V^{-\ell}
, using (
2.3
) and (
2.20
) one finds that for sufficiently small
h
,

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V>0\Rightarrow\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}>0.

As a slightly weaker assumption we assume that
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
, which holds for a class of expanding surfaces (from the explanation above), but this also allows the possibility that
\Gamma(t)
is a stationary surface.

As seen in the previous section, we consider a set of admissible initial conditions, given by

\mathcal{I}_{h,0}:=\left\{\eta_{h}\in S_{h}(0)\mid|\eta_{h}|\leq 1\ \text{a.e. on }\Gamma_{h}(0),\ E^{\text{CH}}_{h}[\eta_{h};0]<\infty,\ \left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(0)}\eta_{h}\right|<1\right\},

where

(4.1)

\displaystyle E^{\text{CH}}_{h}[U_{h};t]=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\frac{\varepsilon|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}U_{h}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}I_{h}F(U_{h})

is a discrete version of the Ginzburg-Landau functional.
We note that the assumption
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
implies that
\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{h}(0)|}\geq\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{h}(t)|}
and so the condition on the mean value is simpler than that for
\mathcal{I}_{0}
defined in Section
3
.

Discretising in space gives rise to our semi-discrete scheme, where the solution spaces in which we pose the problem are

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{h}^{T}:=\left\{z_{h}\in C(\mathcal{S}_{h,T})\mid z_{h}(\cdot,t)\in S_{h}(t)\right\},\quad S_{h}^{T}:=\left\{z_{h}\in\tilde{S}_{h}^{T}\mid\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{z_{h}}\in C(\mathcal{S}_{h,T})\right\}.

The semi-discrete problem is as follows.
Given initial data
U_{h,0}\in\mathcal{I}_{h,0}
, approximating some
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
, we want to find
(U_{h},W_{h})\in S_{h}^{T}\times\tilde{S}_{h}^{T}
such that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h},\phi_{h}\right)+g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}\phi_{h}),1)+a_{h}(W_{h},\phi_{h})=0,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}(W_{h},\phi_{h})=\varepsilon a_{h}(U_{h},\phi_{h})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}(I_{h}f(U_{h}),\phi_{h})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}(U_{h},\phi_{h}),

for all
\phi_{h}(t)\in S_{h}(t)
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, satisfying the initial condition
U_{h}(0)=U_{h,0}
a.e. in
\Gamma_{h}(0)
.
We note that unlike the schemes of
[
11
,
32
,
35
]
we have used mass lumping to control the nonlinearity, as in
[
7
,
21
]
.
This allows us to establish
\delta
-independent bounds on the nonlinearity, which otherwise proves difficult.
Lastly we observe that the
g_{h}
term in (
4.2
) is motivated by Proposition
2.29
, and will allow us to retain a mass conservation property as taking
\phi_{h}=1
above yields

0=\bar{m}_{h}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h},1\right)+g_{h}(U_{h},1)=\frac{d}{dt}\bar{m}_{h}(U_{h},1)=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}U_{h}.

4.2.
Well-posedness of finite element problem

4.2.1.
Regularisation

To show well-posedness of this problem we consider a regularisation as in
[
14
,
15
]
.
The regularised semi-discrete problem is to find a pair
(U_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta})\in S_{h}^{T}\times\tilde{S}_{h}^{T}
solving

(4.4)

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}},\phi_{h}\right)+g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}\phi_{h}),1)+a_{h}(W_{h}^{\delta},\phi_{h})=0,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}(W_{h}^{\delta},\phi_{h})=\varepsilon a_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},\phi_{h})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}(I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),\phi_{h})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},\phi_{h}),

for all
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, along with the initial condition
U_{h}^{\delta}(0)=U_{h,0}
.
As before, we require that
U_{h,0}\in\mathcal{I}_{h,0}
.

As part of the existence proof we will make use of a discrete and regularised version of the Ginzburg-Landau functional (
1.3
),

E^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}\left[U_{h}^{\delta};t\right]:=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\varepsilon\frac{|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}U_{h}^{\delta}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}I_{h}F^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}).

The proof of well posedness is quite long, and hence separated into several parts.
First we prove global existence of a solution to the regularised problem, (
4.4
). Then show uniform bounds, independent of
\delta
so we may pass to the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
.
We then verify that the solution is unique by showing a stability result.
As part of this well posedness, we must show that the solution is such that
|U_{h}|<1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{h}(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, due to the poles of
f
at
\pm 1
.

Throughout we consider initial data
U_{h,0}\in\mathcal{I}_{h,0}
approximating some
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
.
Namely, for a given
u_{0}
we consider
U_{h,0}
such that

\left\|u_{0}-U_{h,0}^{\ell}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{0})}+h\left\|u_{0}-U_{h,0}^{\ell}\right\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})}\leq Ch^{2},

where
C
is independent of
h
but may depend on
u_{0}
.
This allows us to make bounds involving
U_{h,0}
independent of
h
by lifting onto
\Gamma_{0}
.
Suitable examples, for sufficiently smooth
u_{0}
, are the Lagrange interpolant and the Ritz projection.

Lemma 4.3
.

Given
h\in(0,h_{0})
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and
U_{h,0}\in\mathcal{I}_{h,0}
there exists a unique solution pair
(U_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta})\in S_{h}^{T}\times\tilde{S}_{h}^{T}
, solving (
4.2
), (
4.3
) for all
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
and
t\in[0,T]
, along with the initial condition
U_{h}^{\delta}(0)=U_{h,0}
.

Moreover, this pair is such that

(4.6)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}E^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}(t)]+\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}W_{h}^{\delta}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\,dt\leq C,

where
C
is a constant independent of
\delta
and
h
.

Proof.

We firstly show the short time existence of the functions
U_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta}
.
We enumerate the finite element basis functions as
(\phi_{j}(t))_{j=1}^{N_{h}}
and express the functions
U_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta}
as

U_{h}^{\delta}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{h}}\alpha_{j}^{\delta}(t)\phi_{j}(t),\quad W_{h}^{\delta}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{h}}\beta_{j}^{\delta}(t)\phi_{j}(t).

Then the system of ODEs above can be written as

\displaystyle\bar{M}(t)\frac{d}{dt}\alpha^{\delta}(t)+\bar{G}(t)\alpha^{\delta}(t)+A(t)\beta^{\delta}(t)=0,

\displaystyle\bar{M}(t)\beta^{\delta}(t)=\varepsilon A(t)\alpha^{\delta}(t)+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(t;\alpha^{\delta}(t))-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{M}(t)\alpha^{\delta}(t).

Here
\alpha^{\delta},\beta^{\delta}
are the vectors of coefficients, and the other terms are given by

\displaystyle\bar{M}_{ij}(t)=\bar{m}_{h}(\phi_{i}(t),\phi_{j}(t)),\quad\bar{G}_{ij}(t)=g_{h}(I_{h}(\phi_{i}(t)\phi_{j}(t)),1),\quad A_{ij}(t)=a_{h}(\phi_{i}(t),\phi_{j}(t)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(t;\alpha^{\delta}(t))_{j}=\bar{m}_{h}\left(I_{h}f^{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}\alpha_{i}^{\delta}(t)\phi_{i}(t)\right),\phi_{j}(t)\right)=\bar{m}_{h}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}f^{\delta}(\alpha_{i}^{\delta}(t))\phi_{i}(t),\phi_{j}(t)\right).

This system is then subject to the initial condition,
\alpha^{\delta}(0)=\alpha_{0},

where
U_{h,0}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{h}}\alpha_{0,j}\phi_{j}(0)
.
We note that the use of mass-lumping here means that both the matrices
\bar{M}(t),\bar{G}(t)
are diagonal.
Noting that
\bar{M}(t)
is positive definite, we can decouple the equations for a single ODE for
\alpha^{\delta}(t)
.
As
f^{\delta}
is
C^{1}
by construction we observe that
\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(t;\cdot)
is locally Lipschitz, and we can apply standard ODE theory to obtain short time existence of a unique
\alpha^{\delta}
and
\beta^{\delta}
.

Next we show that
U_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta}
exist on
[0,T]
by showing the bound (
4.6
).
We start by differentiating the regularised energy for

\frac{d}{dt}E^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}]=\varepsilon a_{h}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}b_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},U_{h}^{\delta})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}\left(I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}\left(U_{h}^{\delta},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right)\\
+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}g_{h}\left(I_{h}F^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),1\right),

where we have used Proposition
2.17
and Lemma
2.29
.
Now testing (
4.4
) with
W_{h}^{\delta}
, and (
4.5
) with
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}
we see

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta}\right)=-g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}W_{h}^{\delta}),1)-a_{h}(W_{h}^{\delta},W_{h}^{\delta}),

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}\left(W_{h}^{\delta},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right)=\varepsilon a_{h}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta}\right)+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}\left(I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}\left(U_{h}^{\delta},\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right),

from which we obtain

(4.7)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}]+\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}W_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}+g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}W_{h}^{\delta}),1)=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}b_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},U_{h}^{\delta})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}g_{h}\left(I_{h}F^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),1\right).

It is straightforward to see from the smoothness assumption on
V
that

\frac{\varepsilon}{2}b_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},U_{h}^{\delta})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}g_{h}\left(I_{h}F^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),1\right)\leq C+CE^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}].

All that remains is to deal with the
g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}W_{h}^{\delta}),1)
term, and this is where we use the assumption that
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
.
Returning to the matrix formulation, we see that
g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}W_{h}^{\delta}),1)=\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\beta^{\delta}
, which we can write independent of
\beta^{\delta}
as

\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\beta^{\delta}=\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\bar{M}^{-1}\left[\varepsilon A\alpha^{\delta}+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{M}\alpha^{\delta}\right],

where the plan is to show the first and third terms are bounded above (in absolute value) and the second term is bounded below.
Under the assumption that
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
one finds that
\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\bar{M}^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})\geq 0
.
To see this is true we first observe, by definition of
\mathcal{F}^{\delta}
, that we may write
\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})=\bar{M}f^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})
, where we abuse notation and understand
f^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})
as a vector with entries
f^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta}_{i})
.
This is justified as

\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(t;\alpha^{\delta}(t))_{j}=\bar{m}_{h}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{h}}f^{\delta}(\alpha_{i}^{\delta}(t))\phi_{i}(t),\phi_{j}(t)\right)=\bar{m}_{h}\left(f^{\delta}(\alpha_{j}^{\delta}(t))\phi_{j}(t),\phi_{j}(t)\right)=(\bar{M}(t)f^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta}(t)))_{j},

since
\bar{m}_{h}(\phi_{i}(t),\phi_{j}(t))=0
for
i\neq j
.
Hence we find that

\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\bar{M}^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})=\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}f^{\delta}(\alpha^{\delta})=g_{h}(I_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})),1)\geq 0

where we have used the fact that
rf^{\delta}(r)\geq 0
and the assumption
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
.
It remains to show upper bounds on the other terms, namely
\varepsilon\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\bar{M}^{-1}A\alpha^{\delta}
and
\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\alpha^{\delta}
.
We firstly consider the second of these since it is more obvious.
By definition

\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}{\alpha}^{\delta}=g_{h}(I_{h}((U_{h}^{\delta})^{2}),1),

and using the smoothness of
V
one finds

|g_{h}(I_{h}((U_{h}^{\delta})^{2}),1)|\leq C\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{h,t}^{2}\leq C+CE^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}],

where we have used the Poincaré inequality
3
3

3

As is typical for the Cahn-Hilliard equation, we also have used the mass conservation property
\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}U_{h}^{\delta}(t)=\int_{\Gamma_{h}(0)}U_{h,0}
, which follows from testing (
4.4
) with
\phi_{h}=1
.
.

The more involved term is
\varepsilon\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\bar{M}^{-1}A\alpha^{\delta}
.
For this we require two new objects.
Firstly, we define
\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}\in S_{h}(t)
to be the unique solution of

\bar{m}_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},\phi_{h})=a_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},\phi_{h}),

for all
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
.
We note that such a function is bounded with

(4.8)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}\|_{h,t}\leq\frac{C}{{h}}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

where we have used an inverse inequality, and Young’s inequality.
If we denote the vector of nodal values of
\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}
by
\widetilde{\alpha^{\delta}}
, one finds that
\widetilde{\alpha^{\delta}}=\bar{M}^{-1}{A}{\alpha^{\delta}}
, and hence we may write

\varepsilon\alpha^{\delta}\cdot\bar{G}\bar{M}^{-1}A\alpha^{\delta}=\varepsilon g_{h}(I_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}U_{h}^{\delta}),1).

Next we introduce the
L^{2}
projection onto
S_{h}(t)
.
For
\psi\in L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
we define
\Lambda_{h}(\psi)\in S_{h}(t)
to be the unique solution of

m_{h}(\Lambda_{h}(\psi),\phi_{h})=m_{h}(\psi,\phi_{h}),

for all
\phi_{h}\in S_{h}(t)
.
If one has that
\psi\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
then it is known (see
[
37
,
32
]
) that since our triangulation is uniformly quasi-uniform

(4.9)

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{h}\psi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

(4.10)

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{h}\psi-\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq Ch\|\psi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

for constants independent of
t
.
To bound
g_{h}(I_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}U_{h}^{\delta}),1)
we write

|g_{h}(I_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}U_{h}^{\delta}),1)|\leq|g_{h}(I_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}U_{h}^{\delta}),1)-g_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta})|+|g_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta})-m_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell})|\\
+|m_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell})-m_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},\Lambda_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell}))|+|a_{h}({U_{h}^{\delta}},\Lambda_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell}))|,

where we have used the definition of
\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}
in the last term.
Now we use (
2.28
) and an inverse inequality to see that

|g_{h}(I_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}U_{h}^{\delta}),1)-g_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta})|\leq Ch\|\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\leq C\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}.

Next we use the fact that
V_{h}=I_{h}V
, and (
2.21
) (as well as (
2.3
) where needed) to see that

\displaystyle|g_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta})-m_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell})|

\displaystyle\leq Ch\|\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

where we have again used (
4.8
) for the final inequality.
Finally for the last two terms we use (
4.9
) and (
4.10
), as well as the smoothness of
V
, respectively to see that

\displaystyle|m_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell})-m_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}},\Lambda_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell}))|\leq C\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}

\displaystyle|a_{h}({U_{h}^{\delta}},\Lambda_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot V)^{-\ell})|\leq C\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}.

All in all, using these bounds in conjunction with the Poincaré inequality one obtains a bound of the form

|g_{h}(I_{h}(\widetilde{U_{h}^{\delta}}U_{h}^{\delta}),1)|\leq C+CE^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}].

Combining these facts together, and using the Poincaré inequality as appropriate, in (
4.7
) yields

(4.11)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}]+\|\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}W_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\leq C+CE^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h}^{\delta}],

where one concludes by using a Grönwall inequality.

Lastly note
E^{\text{CH},\delta}_{h}[U_{h,0}]
can be bounded independent of
h
by taking lifts onto the surface
\Gamma_{0}
and using
\left\|u_{0}-U_{h,0}^{\ell}\right\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})}\leq Ch
, for some function
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
.
It remains to see that the bound is independent of
\delta
.
For this we note that, by the definition of
F^{\delta}

\int_{\Gamma_{h}(0)}I_{h}F^{\delta}(U_{h,0})=\int_{\{|U_{h,0}|<1-\delta\}}I_{h}F_{\log}(U_{h,0})+\int_{\{U_{h,0}\geq 1-\delta\}}\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{F_{\log}^{(k)}(1-\delta)}{k!}I_{h}\left((U_{h,0}-1+\delta)^{k}\right)\\
+\int_{\{U_{h,0}\leq-1+\delta\}}\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{F_{\log}^{(k)}(-1+\delta)}{k!}I_{h}\left((U_{h,0}+1-\delta)^{k}\right)+\int_{\Gamma_{h}(0)}\frac{1-I_{h}\left((U_{h,0})^{2}\right)}{2}

and so we bound these first three terms.
Since
U_{h,0}\in\mathcal{I}_{h,0}
, we see
|U_{h,0}|\leq 1
, and one clearly has

\int_{\{|U_{h,0}|<1-\delta\}}I_{h}F_{\log}(U_{h,0})\leq|\Gamma_{h}(0)|\sup_{r\in[-1,1]}F_{\log}(r)<\infty,

and for the terms involving derivatives one finds that

\int_{\{U_{h,0}\geq 1-\delta\}}\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{F_{\log}^{(k)}(1-\delta)}{k!}I_{h}(U_{h,0}-1+\delta)^{k}\\
\leq\frac{|\Gamma_{h}(0)|}{2}\left(\sup_{r\in[-1,1]}F_{\log}(r)+\delta\log\left(\frac{2-\delta}{\delta}\right)+\frac{4\delta^{2}}{2\delta-\delta^{2}}\right),

which is finite uniformly bounded for
\delta\in(0,1)
.
The bound for the integral over
\{U_{h,0}\leq-1+\delta\}
is similar.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

The assumption
\nabla_{\Gamma_{h}}\cdot V_{h}\geq 0
was required here as adapting the argument of
[
15
]
would be analogous to taking the limit
h\rightarrow 0
then
\delta\rightarrow 0
.
We expect that one can drop this assumption and expand the analysis presented here to a wider class of evolving surfaces.

We use this bound to show a uniform bound for
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}
in
L^{2}_{H^{-1}}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For
\delta\in(0,1)
we have that

\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right\|_{H^{-1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\leq C

for a constant
C
independent of
\delta
and
h
.

Proof.

Firstly we note that
\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}
does not have mean value 0, and hence we cannot immediately use an inverse Laplacian.
We do know however that it has a bounded mean value, as taking
\phi_{h}=1
in (
4.4
) one finds

\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}=g_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},1)\leq C\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))},

from which one can use (
4.6
) to see that

(4.12)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right)^{2}\leq C.

Now by the definition of
\bar{\mathcal{G}}_{S_{h}}
from Appendix
A
one tests (
4.4
) with
\bar{\mathcal{G}}_{S_{h}}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right)
for

\left\|\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right\|_{-h}^{2}=-a_{h}\left(W_{h}^{\delta},\bar{\mathcal{G}}_{S_{h}}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right)\right)\\
-g_{h}\left(I_{h}\left(U_{h}^{\delta}\bar{\mathcal{G}}_{S_{h}}\left(\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right)\right),1\right).

Now using Young’s inequality, Poincaré’s inequality and (
4.6
) it follows that

(4.13)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right\|_{-h}^{2}\leq C.

Next, by using (
A.1
), (
A.3
), (
A.4
) one finds that

\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}\right\|_{H^{-1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right)^{2}+C\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\partial^{\bullet}_{h}{U_{h}^{\delta}}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\partial^{\bullet}_{h}U_{h}^{\delta}\right\|_{-h}^{2},

and the result follows from (
4.12
), (
4.13
).
∎

Next we state a result showing that one can control the measure of the set of values such that
|U_{h}^{\delta}|>1
.
For brevity’s sake we do not prove this, as it follows from minor adaptions to
[
14
, Lemma 5.8]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

There exists a constant,
C
, independent of
h,\delta
such that

(4.14)

\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}[-1-U_{h}^{\delta}(t)]_{+}+\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}[U_{h}^{\delta}(t)-1]_{+}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{|\log(\delta)|}+\delta\right),

for all
t\in[0,T]
, where
[f]_{+}:=\max(0,f)
.

We now use this control to obtain uniform bounds for the potential term by adapting a proof in
[
21
]
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For sufficiently small
\delta
we have that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\left\|I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\leq C,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}\leq C,

where C denotes a constant independent of
\delta
and
h
.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
, part 1
.

To show the first claim we test (
4.5
) with
\phi_{h}=I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})
to obtain

\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}\left(I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right)=\bar{m}_{h}\left(W_{h}^{\delta},I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right)\\
-\varepsilon a_{h}\left(U_{h}^{\delta},I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bar{m}_{h}\left(U_{h}^{\delta},I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right).

It is a straightforward computation to see that

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{h}\left(I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}),I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right)=\left\|I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right\|_{h,t}^{2}.

Now recalling the assumption that we have an evolving acute triangulation of
\Gamma_{h}(t)
we use (
2.23
) to see that
a_{h}(U_{h}^{\delta},I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta}))\geq 0
, and hence

\left\|I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right\|_{h,t}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\theta}\bar{m}_{h}\left(U_{h}^{\delta},I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right)\\
+\frac{2\varepsilon}{\theta}\bar{m}_{h}\left(W_{h}^{\delta}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}W_{h}^{\delta},I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right).

Now by using a Young’s inequality, and (
2.24
) one finds that

\left\|I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})\right\|_{h,t}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{\theta^{2}}\|U_{h}^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}+\frac{4\varepsilon^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\left\|W_{h}^{\delta}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}W_{h}^{\delta}\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{h}(t))}^{2}.

Now by using the Poincaré inequality, integrating over
[0,T]
and using the bounds from (
4.6
) proves the first result.
∎

To prove the second result one first shows a uniform bound on
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}I_{h}f^{\delta}(U_{h}^{\delta})
, and this requires the following preliminary result.

Lemma 4.8

(
[
21
, Lemma 2.4]
)
.

Let
\eta_{h}\in L^{1}(\Gamma_{h}(t))
be such that there exist
0<\xi,\xi^{\prime}<1
such that

\displaystyle\left|\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}\eta_{h}\right|<1-\xi,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{h}(t)|}\left(\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}[-1-\eta_{h}]_{+}+\int_{\Gamma_{h}(t)}[\eta_{h}-1]_{+}\right)<\xi^{\prime}.

We define the sets

\Gamma_{h,\xi}^{+}(t):=\left\{x\in\Gamma_{h}(t)\mid\eta_{h}>1-\frac{\xi}{2}\right\},\quad\Gamma_{h,\xi}^{-}(t):=\left\{x\in\Gamma_{h}(t)\mid\eta_{h}<-1+\frac{\xi}{2}\right\}.

Then if
\xi^{\prime}<\frac{\xi^{2}}{4}
we have

\displaystyle|\Gamma_{h,\xi}^{+}(t)|<\left(1-\frac{\xi}{2}\right)|\Gamma_{h}(t)|,\quad|\Gamma_{h,\xi}^{-}(t)|<\left(1-\frac{\xi}{2}\right)|\Gamma_{h}(t)|.

For
\eta_{h}=U_{h}^{\delta}
, we find that the first condition holds as
U_{h,0}\in\mathcal{I}_{h,0}
, and hence by using the mass conservation property
